  On the synthetic outdoor palette of `neutral_rule.py` its anchor at `colors` 7 and 12 was the overcast-sky entry (weight 0.22, chroma 0.0112, chroma weight 0.039), a color that file lists among its neutrals, and one 8-bit step in the sky's blue channel (chroma 0.0100) moved the anchor to a different entry; under the product the anchor is a full-weight entry (foliage at `colors` 12, the red and brick entry at 7) and is the same on both sides of that step (VERIFIED arithmetic, `neutral_rule.txt` part 5).
  **The sectors (R6-DEF-1).** A template is a set of sectors on the Oklch hue circle, each a center and a half-width in degrees, rotated together by one angle `theta`; every template covers 90 degrees of hue in all, so the automatic choice below compares placements and not coverage:

  | Template | Sector centers before rotation | Half-width |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `analogous` | 0 | 45 |
  | `complementary` | 0, 180 | 22.5 |
  | `split-complementary` | 0, 150, 210 | 15 |
  | `triadic` | 0, 120, 240 | 15 |

  A hue's distance to a sector is 0 inside it and the shorter arc to its nearer edge outside; its nearest sector is the one at the least distance, the first in table order on a tie; the move is along that arc by `strength * w` of the distance.
  **The fit.** `theta` minimizes the fit cost, the sum over anchors of the distance from the anchor's hue to its nearest sector, anchors unweighted.
  That cost is piecewise linear in `theta`, with a kink only where an anchor sits on a sector center, on a sector edge, or at the midpoint of the gap between two consecutive sectors, so its minimum lies at one of the rotations `theta = h - f` for an anchor hue `h` and one of those template features `f`; `core/color.ts` evaluates exactly those candidates, anchors in anchor order and features in table order (centers, then edges, then gap midpoints), and takes the first minimum.
  With one anchor the first candidate already costs 0, so the template's first sector is centered on it; with several the rotation is the exact minimum, and anchors that cannot all fit stay where they are.
  **The automatic template (Q4 b).** Under `auto` the template is chosen at resolve time and never stored: each template is fitted as above; the one with the least fit cost wins, and among equals the one whose entries would move least at strength 1, the sum over unpinned, untargeted entries of `weight * w * distance to the nearest sector`, ties in table order.
  With one anchor every template fits it exactly and the entries' movement decides; with two anchors 118 degrees apart, the owner's green frog on a blue background, `triadic` is the only template that fits both and wins outright, while from 120 to 150 degrees apart `split-complementary` fits too and the entries decide (R6-5); with two anchors 60 degrees apart `analogous` and `split-complementary` both fit and the entries between them decide (VERIFIED arithmetic, `experiments/harmony-fit/`, which also checks the fit's exactness against a brute-force search).
  `ResolvedPalette.template` names the choice for the Advanced picker, whose other positions force one template.
- **Recolor.** For an object with target T (Oklch), its LUT row maps each entry through `h := T.h`, `C := T.c * clamp(C_entry / max(C_mean, 0.03), 0.5, 1.5)`, `L := L_entry + (T.l - L_mean)`, where `mean` is the object's palette-mapped mean (its `produced.color.mean` run through the assignment on the CPU); the mean lands on the target for any object, including gray ones, and relative shading survives (LOOK-4, FRAME-1, JUDGE-A-9).
- **Subject clarity.** Inside pixels whose G-buffer id belongs to a subject: simplification radius scales by `1 - subjectClarity` and the detail weight is at least `subjectClarity` (Simplify), the band count becomes `round(mix(bands, 6, subjectClarity))`, grain amplitude scales by `1 - 0.5 subjectClarity`, and the pinned entries above exist; outlines along every object edge keep neighbours from vanishing when they share an entry.
- **Simplify (R4-FRESH-2).** Radius `r = 24 * simplify` master px, applied on the working grid of 9.2 as a label-aware anisotropic Kuwahara of radius `r / 4` working texels, three iterations.
  The master's detail finer than the working grid is added back with weight `d = max(0, 1 - r / 8)`: the first third of the slider fades that detail out while the kernel grows to two working texels, and the rest of the slider widens the kernel to 24 master px.
  Inside subject labels the radius scales by `1 - subjectClarity` and `d` is at least `subjectClarity`.
  0 is the master unfiltered, exactly (a kernel of nothing and `d` = 1; VERIFIED in `experiments/simplify-cost/`), and `subjectClarity` 1 leaves a subject unfiltered at any `simplify`.
  The divisor (4), the grid's rounding (`ceil`, with the partial block's label texel and average of 9.2), the iteration count (3), and the kernel cap (6 working texels) are pipeline constants and part of this meaning (DET-1, R6-DEF-4).
  Round 3 committed the same radius at master size, which costs seconds per rerun; the two differ most in how region borders look before quantization (four-pixel ramps here, one-pixel steps there) and least after it, where band boundaries take the same shapes in the spike's proxy; how it reads on real photos is judged with the defaults at P3b (Q12).
- **Outline.** Width `round(6 * outline)` master px, 0 is none; sources are id edges, depth discontinuities above 5% of the depth range, **creases**, where the surface normal turns by more than 30 degrees (R7-24), and **texture edges**; drawn in the palette's darkest variant at alpha `outline`.
  A texture edge is a zero crossing of a difference of Gaussians (sigma 1.0 and 1.6 working texels) of the simplified luminance, computed once per simplification rerun on the working grid and carried as a signed value in the simplified textures' alpha and from there in the G-buffer's surface attachment.
  A pixel is on a texture edge when that value's zero crossing lies within half the outline width of it (`abs(D) / length(grad D)`, in master px) and the value's slope there is at least 0.04 per working texel, so texture edges are traced, band boundaries are not, and the line's width is the outline width whatever the grid.
  Round 3 ran the difference of Gaussians per frame at sigmas of 1.0 and 1.6 master px over the G-buffer color; region borders of a quarter-grid simplification are four-pixel ramps before quantization, which those sigmas barely see, so the source moved to the grid where the edges are sharp, and one full-screen filter left the per-frame path (R4-FRESH-2).
  A crease is found in pass 4, per pixel, from the normals and ids that the G-buffer's surface attachment already carries, and no texture or channel stores it: the normals texture's four bytes hold the two pairs and the surface attachment's four hold the id, the pair, and the texture-edge value (9.3, R7-24).
  The normals of 9.2 spread a crease over the plane fit's window, about seven depth texels (about 21 master px at 2048), so neighbouring pixels differ by a few degrees across a 30-degree crease, and the rule compares normals a stated span apart instead.
  For a pixel `p` of the pass's grid, with `N(x)` the G-buffer's normal at a point `x`, read bilinearly between pixel centers:
  1. The direction `g` is the direction of greatest change of the normal at `p`, the principal direction of its central differences two pixels each way along both axes.
  2. The span is `s = c (4 d + 2) + h` pixels: `d` is the pass's pixels per depth texel along the long edge (2.99 for a 2048 px master over a depth plane 686 texels long), so that `4 d` covers the fit's reach of 3.5 depth texels and the half texel to the nearest; 2 px cover the half-size normals texture's own filtering; `c` is the camera's scale, 1 for the still and for a loop without orbit and `LOOP_ZOOM` for a loop with orbit; and `h` is half the outline width.
  3. The turn across the span, the angle between `N(p - s g)` and `N(p + s g)`, is more than 30 degrees.
  4. The crease's sides are flat: the normal turns by less than a quarter of that angle from `p - s g` to `p - 2 s g` and from `p + s g` to `p + 2 s g`, so a surface that bends steadily, whose normal turns as much over every span, draws no line however tightly it bends.
  5. The crease's middle, the point along `g` where the normal is as far in angle from one side as from the other, the zero of `dot(N(x), N(p + s g) - N(p - s g))`, lies within `h` of `p`, so the line is the outline width wide.
  6. Every point the rule reads lies in a pixel that carries `p`'s own id; otherwise the source draws nothing at `p`.
  The last condition keeps the source within one layer: it draws nothing where its reads would cross an id edge, up to two spans from one along `g`, where the id source already draws the edge and where an object's rim normals, which no derivation from a soft depth gets right (9.2), would otherwise draw a second line inside the silhouette; a crease that meets an id edge squarely is not cut by this condition, since `g` then runs along the edge, but on the back layer it ends where the dilated hole begins, about 1% of the long edge from the silhouette, because the background pair there comes from the fill, which has no crease (9.2).
  Where a strong crease, roughly 55 degrees or more at `relief` 1 and less at higher relief, runs into an object, the step at the hole's edge between the surface and the hole's plane, fitted over a ring that spans both sides of the crease, can itself meet the rule and draw a line along the dilated hole's edge, about 1% of the long edge off the silhouette (INFERRED from the final audit's simulation of R7-32's fill: a 70-degree crease left a 47.8-degree step at `relief` 1, a 50-degree one 24.2); the owner's eye on J1 and J5 judges it, and the rule changes only if such a line shows.
  Two creases farther apart than about three spans each draw their own line; closer ones draw one line, none, or a line of another width, a limit set by normals that spread each crease over seven depth texels.
  The angle is that of the rendered normals, so it follows `relief`: on the simulation's planes a crease of 20 degrees at `relief` 1 measured 37.5 at 2 and drew, and one of 35 measured 19.9 at 0.5 and did not.
  The 30 degrees, the span's terms, the quarter, and the two-pixel differences are pipeline constants and part of this meaning; section 2 records the simulation that settled them (R7-24).
  The earlier text named only "normal angle changes above 30 degrees", which a comparison of neighbouring pixels, its plain reading, can never meet on these normals.
  With symbols on no outline is drawn, because every cell is one color or one glyph (spec section 3).
- **Symbols (R4-FRESH-3).** With `enabled`, the cell grid of `cellSize` (even, 6..48 master px) is centered on the frame, and each cell takes three facts from its pixels: the **majority label**; the **majority variant**, which is the most frequent palette entry among the cell's pixels of that label and then the most frequent band of that entry; and the **tone**, the mean over the same pixels of the lit lightness before banding, `L_entry - s(L_entry) + 0.45 * (t' - 0.55)` with `t'` taken before its clamp and `s` the band window's slide of Bands, which this removes again (R7-B7) (for an unlit scene this is the simplified pixel's own lightness), normalized by the asset's `p01`..`p99` and clamped to 0..1.
  A cell becomes a glyph when its label says `always`, or when it does not say `never`, `amount > 0`, and `abs(tone - 0.5) >= 0.5 (1 - amount)`; at `amount` 0 the slider makes no glyph, so only `always` cells are glyphs, and at `amount` 1 every cell that is not `never` is one.
  Glyph density is five equal bins over the slider's own window (R5-AUDIT-1): with `e = abs(tone - 0.5)` and `u = (e - 0.5 (1 - amount)) / (0.5 amount)`, the glyph is the one of `.` `-` `+` `*` `#` at index `min(4, floor(5 u))`, sparsest at the window's inner edge and densest at the extreme, deeper shadow or brighter highlight being denser.
  An `always` cell whose tone lies inside the window's inner edge (`u` below 0), and every `always` cell at `amount` 0, takes the sparsest glyph.
  Every `amount` above 0 therefore shows all five glyphs in order (VERIFIED arithmetic, `experiments/glyph-rule/density.txt`); bins of `e` over the whole tone range would show only `*` and `#` at the default `amount` 0.3.
  Ink and paper are two adjacent bands of the cell's entry, in the LUT row of the cell's label, and the ink is always the one nearer the extreme: where the cell's band has a neighbor toward the extreme, the paper is the cell's own variant and the ink is that neighbor; where the cell is already in the extreme band, the ink is the cell's own variant and the paper is the neighbor toward the midtones.
  `bands` is at least 2 and an entry's variants are strictly ordered (Bands), so ink and paper always differ, a denser glyph always reads as more extreme, and glyph cells stay inside the palette (FIT-4).
  Round 3 put the ink one band toward the extreme in every case, and that band does not exist exactly where the slider puts symbols first: on a gray ramp at the defaults all 82 glyph cells had ink equal to paper, and `amount` 0 still drew 6 (`experiments/glyph-rule/`).
  A solid cell is one flat block of its majority variant; grain runs after the grid.
- **Grain.** Luma noise of amplitude `0.12 * grain` in L and chroma noise of amplitude `0.06 * colorNoise` in (a, b), per `grainSize` px cell of the output grid, from an integer hash of (seed, `floor(frame / grainHold) mod ceil(frameCount / grainHold)`, cell), the still taking frame 0 (R5-OBS-5); 0 is none; `grainHold` above 1 holds each grain frame for that many loop frames, which is what lets H.264 carry it (EXPORT-1; measured in `h264_grain4.txt`, section 14).
  For the `master` output the grid is the master grid, so preview, PNG, and MP4 share cells; for a GIF the grid is the GIF's own (Q8).
- **Clipping.** Lightness above `k = 1 - 0.35 * clipping` compresses to 1 through a knee of width 0.05; 0 is none.
- **Crunch.** 8 x 8 block DCT on luma and on 2 x 2-subsampled chroma, blocks anchored at the master origin, quantized by the JPEG luma table scaled from quality 100 at `crunch` 0 to quality 20 at `crunch` 1, then inverse; 0 is passthrough, exactly, because the passes are skipped; the coefficients are signed and reach about 8 for input in 0..1, so the four passes of each plane run on half-float targets (8.2, R5-FRESH-5); crunch runs after grain so the noise is mangled into blocks the way an early-2000s JPEG engine mangled it.
- **Fry.** `fryCurve(f)` interpolates linearly between the f = 0 tuple (colors 12, saturation 0.2, simplify 0.1, bands 6, grain 0.1, colorNoise 0.05, crunch 0, clipping 0) and the f = 1 tuple (colors 4, saturation 0.9, simplify 0.8, bands 3, grain 0.8, colorNoise 0.5, crunch 0.7, clipping 0.5), rounding the integers; the `fry` edit writes those absolute values, so the stored document's meaning never depends on the curve and retuning the curve changes no saved scene (FIT-1); `fry` itself is the dial's last position for the UI.
- **Motion.** When `effectiveOrbit` is 0 (motion off, or `orbit` 0 with objects moving) the loop camera is the identity, so the loop keeps the still's framing (Q19 a); otherwise the camera zooms by `LOOP_ZOOM` and translates on a circle of radius `orbit * parallaxClamp` in the image plane and keeps `anchorDisparity` fixed; displacement of a point at disparity d is `offset * (d - anchor) * gain` with `gain` chosen so the farthest point from the anchor moves by exactly the clamp, which never exceeds `PARALLAX_MARGIN`, the margin `LOOP_ZOOM` hides (R3-AUDIT-6); `bob` translates by `0.15 * amount * bound.y * height * sin(2 pi t)`, `sway` rotates about the bottom center of the bounds by the angle whose top arc equals `0.10 * amount * bound.x * height * sin(2 pi t)`, `wobble` adds two sines in x and y at 2 and 3 cycles with amplitude `0.03 * amount * bound * height`; every period is an integer number of cycles per loop and phase is `frame / frameCount` for whichever `frameCount(motion, fps)` the output uses, so frame `frameCount` equals frame 0 at any fps (TEST-9, AUDIT-12), and `bound` shrinks toward 0 as the object's bounds approach the frame edge so no cut edge slides into the crop (JUDGE-A-16).

## 11. Defaults

Applied at scene creation and stored, so changing a default later never alters a saved scene (FIT-3).
The owner shipped this table as recommended (Q12, settled); every value is a slider, and the intents are the design's.

| Setting | Default | Intent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `style.fry` | 0.6 | a visibly fried first look that is not yet destroyed; the curve yields colors 7, saturation 0.62, simplify 0.52, bands 4, grain 0.52, colorNoise 0.32, crunch 0.42, clipping 0.30 |
| `style.harmony` | `{ kind: 'auto', strength: 0.5 }` | colors harmonize out of the box with the template chosen by the anchors (Q4 b); the owner's frog example needs harmony on (FIT-2) |
| `style.paletteTargets` | `[]` | the owner colors an entry by tapping its swatch (Q4 a) |
| `style.outline` | 0.4 | shapes read as outlined without a comic-book border |
| `style.subjectClarity` | 0.6 | the subject visibly sharper than the surround from the moment the auto subject lands |
| `style.grainSize`, `style.grainHold` | 2, 2 | grain that survives H.264 at the default bitrate: measured 46.9% retention at 28 Mbps on the Mac, 46.5% with the default crunch (`h264_grain4.txt`; Q7, settled, with the bitrate slider) |
| `symbols` | off, cellSize 12, amount 0.3 | symbols are an opt-in feature group |
| `motion` | off, orbit 0.6, loopSeconds 3, focus null | motion is opt-in; a first orbit that clearly parallaxes without straining the plate |
| `lighting` | ambient 0.7, relief 1 | adding a first light brightens rather than plunging the scene dark |
| new object | subject false (true when none exists, Q5 b), recolor none, symbols slider, motion none, thickness 0.3, name "Object N"; `selection.candidate` 0 | |
| new light | depth 0.8, color white (l 0.95, c 0, h 0), intensity 1, radius 0.6, rays 0.3, shadows on, softness 0.3 | a near, soft key light with visible rays; white, so it lifts tone bands and leaves the palette's chroma alone (R5-FRESH-2); the picker's chroma runs 0..0.25 (R6-OBS-2) |
| `seed` | random uint32 at import | grain differs per scene, deterministic thereafter |

## 12. Photo import (`photo/`)

- Dimensions first (R5-FRESH-7): the photo's oriented size is read from an `HTMLImageElement` loaded from an object URL of the file, which the page never draws or decodes, and whose `naturalWidth` and `naturalHeight` follow the file's orientation; the URL is revoked as soon as they are read.
  The limits below, the no-upscale rule, and the master size are fixed from that size before any decode: for an oriented photo of long edge `L` and short edge `S`, the master's long edge is `min(2048, L)` floored to even, and its short edge is `S` times that long edge over `L`, rounded to the nearest even pixel by `2 * Math.round(x / 2)`, so an exact odd value goes up; both resize options are passed, so `master.size` is this design's arithmetic and not a browser's rounding (R7-C).
  Both sides are even because an H.264 encoder configured at a size other than a frame's visible size scales the frame (the WebCodecs specification: "The encoder MUST scale any VideoFrame whose [[visible width]] differs from this value"), and that scaling would resample every grain cell and crunch block that sections 8 and 21 keep on the output grid; WebKit's `encode` ignores `visibleRect`, and in Chrome 154 a 2048 x 1365 frame into a 2048 x 1364 encoder came back with a middle-row error of 18.76 against 0.75 for an even control (section 2).
  A 3:2 crop of a 4032 px photo, 2048 x 1365.3, becomes 2048 x 1366, and a 1500 x 1125 photo becomes 1500 x 1126.
  `parseDocument` checks both sides, so a document from another build or a hand-made fixture with an odd side fails `invalid` and never reaches the encoder (5.2, R7-20).
  This is a committed meaning, changed at version 0 where it costs nothing (Q18); round 6 rounded the short edge to the nearest pixel, which made it odd about half the time.
  An image element that fails to load is the decode failure of the HEIC bullet below.
  INFERRED: that both browsers report the oriented size, and that reading it does not make WebKit hold a full decode; P2d.1 imports an EXIF-rotated JPEG in both browsers and a HEIC original in Mac Safari, the only path by which a HEIC original enters, and U6's 48 MP row records the second.
- Decode with `createImageBitmap(file, { imageOrientation: 'from-image', colorSpaceConversion: 'default', resizeWidth, resizeHeight, resizeQuality: 'high' })` sized to the master, so the page never owns a full-resolution bitmap; when the options are rejected, decode at full size and downscale through `drawImage`, closing the full bitmap at once (PHONE-8; the resize path is VERIFIED on the Mac, its internal decode size on WebKit is U6).
  `decodePhoto` reports which path ran, and `import.done` records it (`path: 'resize' | 'fallback'`, 5.10, R7-D9); both browsers accept the resize options, so the fallback runs only when a row rejects them by injection.
- HEIC: Safari decodes it natively, Chrome does not; when decoding fails and the bytes carry an HEIF brand (`ftypheic`, `heix`, `mif1`, `msf1`), import returns `heic-unsupported` and the UI says "Chrome can't open HEIC photos. Open noisy in Safari, or export the photo as JPEG." (Q10, settled).
- Limits: a long edge under 512 px is `too-small`, and so is a photo whose master short side, by the rule above, would be under 64 px (R7-19); above 64 MP is `too-large`.
  The short-side limit keeps every grid derived from the master from degenerating: without it a 4097 x 1 strip rounds its short side to 0, and at 64 px a 2048 px master still has a 32-texel mask and label texture, a 16-texel working grid, eight rows of crunch blocks, one whole cell at the largest cell size, a 15 px GIF at the default 480 px, an 8 px thumbnail, and a 3360 x 98 depth input inside the token bound of 5.2.
  `too-small` carries the oriented size and which limit refused it (5.9); the long-edge limit is checked first, so a photo under both, such as 511 x 30, carries `long-edge`.
  The Library shows one plain sentence for each refusal, naming its limit, in the manner of the HEIC sentence above (R7-28):
  - `too-small` by the long edge: "This photo is too small. noisy needs at least 512 pixels on its long side."
  - `too-small` by the master short side: "This photo is too narrow. Fitted within 2048 pixels, its short side would be under the 64 pixels noisy needs."
  - `too-large`: "This photo is too large. noisy opens photos of up to 64 megapixels."
- Master: PNG through `OffscreenCanvas.convertToBlob`.
  PNG is kept over JPEG because a PNG decodes to identical pixels in every browser, and the palette statistics, the masks, the depth, and the look are all computed from those pixels; a JPEG master would let two devices analyze slightly different images (ENG-10's benefit question, answered; the cost is about 3.4 to 4 MB per master and about 0.5 s of encode on the phone, measured 95 to 114 ms on the Mac).
- Source: the picked file's bytes are stored verbatim as an asset and never decoded again (Q2, settled); the master stays the canonical grid, and the Library's Drop original frees the bytes per scene.
  On the phone those bytes are what the Photos picker delivers, a re-encoded JPEG with auxiliary data stripped (the bench photo arrived as 3,971,900 bytes for 24 MP, about 1.3 bits per pixel; `research-ml-runtimes.md` section 3, WebKit bug 207088), so a phone scene's source is not the HEIC original; originals arrive only through the Files app or a Mac import (FRESH-13, stated in Q2).
- Statistics on a 128 px copy: k-means with k = 16 (k-means++ with a fixed seed, 20 iterations) in Oklab and tone percentiles; tens of milliseconds; pure `photo/stats.ts`, with the sRGB to Oklab conversions of `core/color.ts`, which P2d.1 creates for it and P3b.1 extends (R7-3).
  The seeding takes only distinct colors as centers and a cluster that ends with no pixel is dropped, so `analysis.colors` holds 1 to 16 clusters, every one of weight above 0, and a photo with fewer than 16 distinct colors (a screenshot, a flat photo, a uniform test fixture) yields fewer (R6-2); padding to 16 was rejected because a weight-0 cluster is an entry no pixel maps to, which Ward merging, the swatch strip, and a palette target would each have to special-case.
- Thumbnail: a 256 px JPEG of the master, written into the scenes record at creation as the placeholder until the first render on close; it is a record field, not an asset, so nothing references it and no sweep can take it (R4-FRESH-7).
- Order: the source and the master are hashed and their blobs built first; then `store.createScene` writes the source, the master, the record, and the thumbnail in one readwrite transaction over `scenes` and `assets` (R4-FRESH-8).
  A crash, a killed tab, or a quota failure at any point leaves nothing, so no state with assets and no record exists and nothing has to sweep for one; the import's error names quota when that is the cause.
  VERIFIED in Chrome (`experiments/idb-atomic-import/`): an aborted transaction carrying a 50 MB blob, and one whose last request failed, each left no record and no asset, and the committed one took 75 ms; INFERRED for WebKit from the IndexedDB specification, and checked by P2b under `safaridriver` and by P2d's import on the phone.
- Target: first look within 2 s of picking on the phone, composed of decode and resize (0.1 to 0.2 s), PNG encode (about 0.5 s), statistics, one store transaction, session open, and the first low-mode render with the programs compiled at boot (9.1).

## 13. Persistence, scene files, and devices

### 13.1 IndexedDB

Database `noisy`, schema version 1:

| Store | Key | Value |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `scenes` | `id` | `{ id, doc, updatedAt, thumbnail: Blob | null, lastExportedAt: string | null }`, index `updatedAt` |
| `assets` | `[sceneId, assetId]` | `{ sceneId, assetId, mediaType, blob }` |
| `caches` | `[sceneId, key]` | `{ bytes: ArrayBuffer, usedAt, model }`, key `sam-embed:<master asset>` ; LRU by bytes bounded at `embeddingsCacheBytes` (400 MB, about 24 scenes), because every eviction costs the phone another run of the budget-defining encoder (JUDGE-A-17, FRESH-12) |
| `device` | `'state'` | `DeviceState` |

Connection: `store/connection.ts` holds at most one `IDBDatabase`, drops it on `close`, `versionchange`, `UnknownError`, or `InvalidStateError`, reopens on the next operation, and retries a failed operation once on a fresh handle; a second failure maps to `unavailable` with the banner "Storage stopped responding; export this scene or reload" while the document stays in memory and each edit retries (PHONE-4, CLAIM-10).
The schema version changes only for object-store or index changes, never for document changes; an open connection handles `versionchange` by closing and reloading the page; `VersionError` on open maps to `newer-version` (PERSIST-4).
One origin per device is part of the data's identity: `https://<mac>.ts.net` today, the AWS domain at release; the dev server prints exactly that URL, and a change of origin is a scene-file export and import.

### 13.2 Save semantics

- Within a session, assets first, always: `putAsset` commits in its own transaction before any edit or produce references the id, and `createObject` or a produce that cannot store its asset fails typed and changes nothing (FAIL-5, JUDGE-A-4).
  There is therefore no unsaved-asset set to lose; the only unsaved state is the document itself.
  Creating a scene is the one write that carries assets and a record together, and it does so in a single transaction (`createScene`: photo import, scene-file import, keep-both, and replace), so there too no record ever references an asset that is not committed (R4-FRESH-8).
- Products save the document immediately after they apply; user edits save after `saveDebounceMs`; every pending save flushes on `visibilitychange` to hidden and on `pagehide` as a document-only write; thumbnails render on close and after an export, never inside the flush (FAIL-6).
- Quota on save: the document stays in memory, editing continues, the session calls `store.evictCaches()` and retries once, the banner says "Storage is full: changes aren't being saved. Export this scene or delete old scenes.", and every later edit retries (A section 15, ENG-8 d).
- `exportScene()` assembles the file from the in-memory document and the session's asset cache, which holds the bytes of every asset the document or its history references (about 15 MB for a typical phone scene, more with a kept 48 MP source), and stamps the file's `writer` from the running build; it reads nothing from the store, so it works while the store is `unavailable` or over quota, and a stale store copy cannot leak into the rescue path (FAIL-2, FRESH-5, TEST-12).
  The asset cache is filled at open (one `getAsset` per referenced id, the same reads that decode the master, depth, plate, and masks) and by every `putAsset` the session performs, whose bytes the caller already holds; the store is a read source only for closed scenes through the Library.
- One orphan sweep, at open (R5-AUDIT-3): `store.sweepOrphans(scene)` runs under the scene lock right after `load`, before the asset cache fills and before the driver's first reconcile, so no asset write can be in flight (FRESH-16).
  The store computes the reference set itself, in the same readwrite transaction, from the stored record (`core.assetRefs`), and deletes only when that record is at the current version and parses; no caller supplies a set, so a sweep can never use references the stored record does not have.
  A record that `load` had to migrate is therefore not swept at that open: a migration may drop a reference the stored JSON still holds (13.4 rule 6), and the sweep waits for a later open, after a save has rewritten the record.
  Nothing is swept while a scene is open or at close.
  The cost is that replaced plates and masks, a few megabytes per editing session, wait for the scene's next open to be reclaimed; a scene's assets are per scene, so deletion is a key-range delete and no reference counting exists.
  At close the session still awaits every in-flight `putAsset` before it releases the lock, and a produce or decode result that arrives after close is dropped; P3c injects a delayed `putAsset` during close and asserts it is awaited.
  Q2's "Drop original" (`dropOriginal`) is the one other caller: it sweeps right after its own save has succeeded, which meets the same condition.
- No Library-level sweep exists (R4-FRESH-8).
  Rounds 2 and 3 added `sweepUnowned`, a per-id lock spanning both import paths from the first asset write through the document save, and a re-check under that lock, all to reclaim or protect the assets of an import that stopped between its asset writes and its record.
  One transaction makes that state unreachable, so the sweep, the lock-spanning rule, the re-check, and their two cross-tab tests are removed; `TestSurface.storeAudit().unownedRanges` stays as the oracle that the state never occurs.
  `delete` removes a record, its asset key range, and its cache entries in one transaction for the same reason.

### 13.3 One writer, and the second tab

The session holds `noisy-scene-<id>` for its lifetime.
`withSceneLock` has one acquisition mode, `ifAvailable`, so nothing ever waits on a scene lock (AUDIT-9): a second tab that opens the same scene gets `open-elsewhere` at once and the Library says "This scene is open in another tab. Close it there first."; there is no read-only editor mode (ENG-9, PERSIST-2).
Library delete, import-replace, Library export (`exportSceneFile`, parsed or verbatim), and `dropOriginal` take the same lock for their duration and fail with the same message, `open-elsewhere` in each one's result type (FAIL-4, R5-FRESH-7).
Photo import and keep-both import create a scene under a new id in one transaction and take no lock: no other tab can see the id before the commit (R4-FRESH-8).
Reopening after the other tab closes needs no mechanism: the owner taps Open again (TEST-19's pass condition is refusal, then success once the other tab has closed).

### 13.4 Scene file and the version rule

A scene file is a zip named `<title>.noisy`:

```
scene.json                       deflate-raw: SceneFileJson (format, writer, document, manifest)
assets/<sha256hex>.png           stored: master, plate
assets/<sha256hex>.jpeg|.heic    stored: the source bytes, when kept
assets/<sha256hex>.bin           deflate-raw: raw planes (depth f32, masks u8)
```

A scene file carries no thumbnail: the thumbnail is a field of each device's scenes record, the manifest is built from the document's references, and the importing device makes its own from the master at import with `photo.thumbnailOf` (R4-FRESH-7).

Writer: entries in a fixed order (`scene.json`, then assets sorted by id), every timestamp from the document's `updatedAt`, sizes and CRC-32 in the local header (no data descriptors), method 0 for PNG, JPEG, and HEIC, method 8 through `CompressionStream('deflate-raw')` for JSON and planes; exporting the same document twice yields identical bytes (TEST-10).

Reader (`scenefile/zip.ts`): locates the End of Central Directory from the file's tail, reads the central directory, slices each entry by its recorded offset and sizes, honors data descriptors by trusting the central directory, skips directory entries, `__MACOSX/`, and `._` entries, strips one common leading directory across all remaining entries, decodes methods 0 and 8 through `DecompressionStream('deflate-raw')`, and refuses encryption, ZIP64, more than 256 entries, a declared total above 512 MB, an inflated entry longer than declared, or more than one leading directory (FILE-1, ENG-4, TEST-11).
The two `ditto` archives in `experiments/zip-rezip/` are design A dummies, not scenes: each `scene.json` is `{"format":"noisy-scene","writer":"test","document":{"formatVersion":1}}`, `assets/aaaa.png` is 200,000 random bytes, and neither asset name is a 64-hex `AssetId`, so they serve only P2c.1's zip-level rows (the leading directory, the `__MACOSX/` and `._` entries, the data descriptors) and could never open as scenes (R7-B4).
The import fixtures are P2c.2's: a minimal scene the project's writer makes, unpacked and re-zipped by the same two `ditto` calls that made those archives (`ditto -c -k --sequesterRsrc --keepParent` on the folder, which adds the leading directory, and the same without `--keepParent` on its contents), committed as frozen bytes beside the script that made them.
They are frozen bytes exempt from the rule that regenerating a fixture yields identical bytes, like the experiment archives, because `ditto` embeds file timestamps; and because `--sequesterRsrc` writes `__MACOSX/` and `._` entries only for files that carry extended attributes, the script writes one on the scene's files before each `ditto` call and asserts that each archive lists a `__MACOSX/` directory and a `._` entry, so the skip rules' coverage is guaranteed and not an accident of the writing process (R7-15).
Every manifest asset is hash-verified on read; a document that references an asset absent from the manifest fails as `corrupt`, and so does one with unknown keys at a known version, with the key paths in the detail (rule 5).
Import validates everything, then writes through `store.createScene` in one transaction (R4-FRESH-8): `create` for a new id and for keep-both, which mints a new id; `replace`, under the scene's `ifAvailable` lock so an open session is never overwritten, when the owner chose to replace.
A document whose `id` already exists asks "Replace the copy on this device or keep both?" and shows both edit times, this device's and the file's, marking the newer; `same-scene-exists` carries `localUpdatedAt` and `incomingUpdatedAt` for it (R4-FRESH-9).
Every laptop-to-phone return hits this prompt, and with one time shown a replace in the wrong direction could discard the newer edits unseen.
The Library's Import scene input sets no `accept` filter: a custom extension in an accept list is a known way to make files unselectable in iOS's file picker (INFERRED; U14 checks the return trip on the phone), and a file that is not a scene fails as `not-a-scene` anyway.

Version rule:

1. `document.format` must equal `noisy-scene` and `document.version` must be a non-negative integer; anything else is `not-a-scene`.
   Version 0 is a valid version so that the development version, which Q18 settled, is readable by the build that writes it (R3-AUDIT-5).
2. `peekVersion(json)` runs before the strict parse in the store and the file reader; a version above `SCENE_VERSION` is `newer-version` with the writer's build id, and the Library lists such a record as `newer`, offering only Export scene file, never Delete and never a rewrite (STATE-2, JUDGE-A-4, JUDGE-B-8).
   That export is the **verbatim path** (AUDIT-4): `loadVerbatim` returns the stored JSON untouched, `listAssets` over the scene's whole key range builds the manifest, and `writeSceneFile` with a `verbatim` payload writes `scene.json` as stored, so the newer build that wrote the record reopens the file and hash-verifies it exactly as it would any file of its own version; this build never needs to understand the document to move it.
3. A version below `SCENE_VERSION` runs `MIGRATIONS[v]` for each v up to `SCENE_VERSION - 1`, each a total function `unknown -> unknown` in `core/migrate.ts`, then the current schema parses the result; any failure lists the record as `damaged` with "Export raw data" first and "Delete" second, where "Export raw data" is the same verbatim path labeled for what it yields (a zip any unzip tool opens, whose stored PNG and JPEG assets are readable even when `scene.json` is not).
   `SCENE_VERSION` is `MIGRATIONS.length` (5.2, R6-DEF-6), so the table walk and the version cannot disagree.
4. Every change to the document's shape or to a committed meaning in section 10 appends one migration to `MIGRATIONS`, which is the version bump, including additive fields, so "same version" means "same shape"; each migration ships with two fixtures a test opens, a document of the old version as its input and the document it must yield as the reference.
   The rule binds from the freeze (Q18, settled): `MIGRATIONS` is empty and the version 0 through development, `MIGRATIONS[0]` becomes the identity onto the frozen shape when the owner declares the first scene worth keeping, which is expected after v1, and a declaration earlier is the owner's call under Q18 and starts the freeze task then (R7-12), and every entry after it is a real migration with its fixtures (FRESH-14); at that freeze the pair captured is the last version-0 scene and its version-1 reading (R6-5).
   **The older-version seam (R6-DEF-6).** No shipped build can read a record older than its own version before the first real migration exists, so the rows that plant one (P2b.2's `sweepOrphans` on a migrated record, P3a.4's open path, `verification.md` E18) run in a **harness build**: `tools/build.mjs --migrations test/fixtures/migrate-synthetic.ts` substitutes that module for `core/migrate.ts` through an esbuild resolve rule, in a dev build only, and the production build refuses the flag.
   The synthetic module exports a two-entry chain, so its `SCENE_VERSION` is 2: `MIGRATIONS[0]` adds `analysis.legacyPlate: ImageRef | null` and `MIGRATIONS[1]` deletes it, so a planted version-1 record naming a planted asset in `legacyPlate` is a migrated record whose migration drops a reference, and a planted version-0 record walks both.
   Nothing else in the build differs, so the store, the codec, and the open path exercise the real code on the synthetic chain; the harness build is driven only in a throwaway browser profile (2.4 of `verification.md`), never against the dev database, whose version-0 records it would list as older and whose records it would write at version 2.
   The same chain has a node seam that needs no build (R6-4): `core/schema.ts` exports the pure factory `parseDocumentWith(migrations)` and binds `parseDocument = parseDocumentWith(MIGRATIONS)` (5.2), so P2a.1's `node --test` row imports the synthetic module and passes its chain to the factory directly, while every production caller imports only the bound `parseDocument`; the browser rows keep the module swap, because the store, the codec, and the open path must run on the chain without any of them taking a table.
5. Unknown keys at a known version make the record `damaged`, with the key paths in the detail (R5-FRESH-9); a missing or invalid required field is `damaged` as before.
   The record is never opened, never rewritten, and never swept, so its stored JSON and every asset in its key range stay as they are; the Library offers "Export raw data" first and "Delete" second, as for any damaged record, and the build that wrote the record opens the exported file.
   A scene file whose document has such keys fails as `corrupt` with the same detail, and nothing is written.
   Once rule 4 binds, a branch switch yields `newer-version`, not unknown keys, so this state needs two parallel branches at one version with different shapes, or a bug, and both devices load the same build from the Mac.
   While the version is 0, as Q18 settled, rule 4 does not bind, and the state is the ordinary result of removing or renaming a field: every existing development scene then lists as damaged, by design, and is made again from its photo (R5-CA-7).
   Section 21 records why the earlier rule, dropping the keys with a notice, was reversed.
6. Asset bytes are self-describing (PNG, JPEG, HEIC, and planes typed by `PlaneRef`), so a migration never rewrites bytes.
   The null-for-recompute path covers `analysis.depth` and `analysis.plate`: a product whose shape changes is set to null by the migration, `reconcile` lists it, and its old asset becomes an orphan (STATE-1, B rule).
   Per-object products (`mask`, `bounds`, `selection`, `color`) have no reconcile need; they are re-runnable from the stored `Selection` through an explicit per-object refine, and a mask whose `PlaneRef.size` differs from the renderer's array size is resampled on upload rather than rewritten (AUDIT-14).
   If an asset rewrite is ever unavoidable, the migration type becomes `(doc, assets: { read(id): Promise<Blob>; write(blob): Promise<AssetId> }) => Promise<SceneDocument>` and runs inside the store's load path and the file import path, never in `core`.
7. The same `parseDocument` chain reads IndexedDB records; a record is rewritten at the current version on its next save.
8. Tool caches (embeddings) never enter the file; the new device rebuilds them on the first tap.
9. Scene-file strings (`title`, `original.name`) are length-bounded by the schema and rendered as text by Preact.

### 13.5 Durability on the phone

- The Library requests `navigator.storage.persist()` on first launch and shows the answer; `persist()` protects against quota eviction, not against Safari's script-writable-storage deletion (CLAIM-6).
- Safari deletes a site's script-writable storage after seven days of Safari use without the owner interacting with noisy (a tap or keystroke, not a scroll); a Home Screen web app added with "Open as Web App" on is exempt and has its own IndexedDB, separate from Safari tabs (CLAIM-7, PHONE-10, STRONGLY SUPPORTED; iOS 26 confirmation is U7).
- When not standalone, the Library shows one persistent line, worded to be true in every browser (R5-OBS-4), whatever `persist()` answered (R5-CA-5).
  With `persisted` false it reads "Scenes here are not protected. The browser can delete them, and Safari does after 7 days away. Export the scenes you want to keep."; with `persisted` true it reads "Safari deletes a site's data after 7 days away. Export the scenes you want to keep."; in both, when `deviceClass` is `phone` the last sentence reads "Export the scenes you want to keep, or add noisy to your Home Screen." (PERSIST-1, FIT-7).
  The line is absent only in the Home Screen app.
  The closing revision showed it only when not persisted, which hid it in a Safari tab that had been granted `persist()`, the one case where the first bullet says the seven-day deletion still applies.
  Safari's seven-day rule applies on the Mac too (STRONGLY SUPPORTED: WebKit's March 2020 post on full third-party cookie blocking names iOS and iPadOS 13.4 and Safari 13.1 on macOS), and a Chrome origin that is not persisted is best-effort storage, so the first wording holds in each; the second says only what is true of Safari, and in a persisted Chrome origin it is shown too, where it is noise, because the app detects no browser and this design adds no detection.
  UNKNOWN: whether Safari grants `persist()` in a tab on iOS 26 or macOS 26 (the bench recorded `persisted` false), and whether a persisted origin is exempt from the seven-day rule; U7 records both, and if either makes the case unreachable the second wording is removed.
  Each scene shows its last export time, and the notice ships in the same package as the first scene write (JUDGE-A-18, JUDGE-B-18).
- Model files follow the same eviction; a re-download needs the Mac reachable and running noisy's start command (Q21), and so does loading the app at all, until the AWS host exists at release: v1 has no service worker and no offline shell (Q11, settled), so the phone opens noisy over Tailscale, picks photos into it, and works on them while the Mac is reachable.
- Scene files in iCloud Drive are the durable backup either way.

## 14. Export

- **PNG still.** `render(still)` into the canvas at master size, `canvas.toBlob('image/png')`, `File("<title>.png")`.
- **MP4 loop.** Gate with `VideoEncoder.isConfigSupported({ codec, width, height, bitrate, framerate: 30, avc: { format: 'avc' } })` at the master size, whose sides are even (section 12), with the level chosen from the size (`avc1.640032` for 2048 x 1536); when the master size is refused, hide MP4 with the reason (R7-C).
  There is no size fallback: round 6's "fall back to the largest supported size at the same aspect" had no render path (`RenderOutput` is `master` or `gif`), so it could only have run through the encoder's own scaling, which the WebCodecs specification makes mandatory for a frame of another visible size ("The encoder MUST scale any VideoFrame whose [[visible width]] differs from this value"), which WebKit leaves to VideoToolbox's stretching scaling mode while ignoring `visibleRect`, and which Chrome 154 measurably did (section 2); that scaling resamples every grain cell and crunch block, which this design forbids for the loop crop on the same grounds (section 21).
  The Macs accept every even master size tried, and WebKit's only H.264 checks are even dimensions and a level cap of 5.2, so the refusal is not expected on the target devices; the deletion stands on U13's phone row in P1.5, which checks `isConfigSupported` at the master sizes on the phone, and a refusal there may reopen a fallback only with a real render path, a `RenderOutput` that runs the camera stage on a video grid as the GIF's does, never with encoder scaling.
  For each frame: `render(loop, frame)` into the canvas, a `VideoFrame` with `{ timestamp: frame * 33333 }` taken by the frame source below, `encoder.encode(frame, { keyFrame: frame % 30 === 0 })`, `frame.close()`, waiting while `encodeQueueSize > 2`; chunks and the decoder configuration go to Mediabunny writing a fast-start MP4 into memory (PHONE-5, JUDGE-B-14: no RGBA readback, no re-upload, the Export tab shows frames as they render).
  **The frame source** (R7-D9) is chosen at the job's first frame from `policy.frameSource` (5.9), a per-device-class constant like the other policy values: `direct`, `new VideoFrame(canvas)` on the WebGL2 `HTMLCanvasElement`; `drawImage`, one `drawImage(canvas)` per frame into a 2D `OffscreenCanvas` and `new VideoFrame(offscreen)`, which the Mac probe VERIFIED, at the cost of one master-size copy per frame; or `readPixels` into an RGBA buffer, the last resort.
  The policy says `direct` until P1.5's U13 row, the first that runs a WebGL2 canvas through the encoder on the phone (FRESH-8), shows it does not make a good frame there; if constructing the first frame by the chosen path throws, the job takes the next path in that order for the whole export, and `export.video.frameSource` records the path the frames used, so V7's comparison with the probe's choice reads one field.
  Round 6 said the choice was made "at runtime by that probe's result recorded in the environment snapshot", which had no such field, no event, and no stated moment; a policy constant also lets the Mac run all three paths through `TestSurface.config({ frameSource })`.
  Bitrate defaults to 0.30 bits per pixel per frame (about 28 Mbps at 2048 x 1536) with a slider capped by `isConfigSupported`.
  An encoder error fails the job with the encoder's message, and the Export tab points at the bitrate slider; nothing is retried at another bitrate, so an MP4 is never silently below the bitrate the owner chose, or below the grain line that follows (R5-FRESH-3).
  Acceptance in P8 on both devices: a decoded flat region retains at least 40% of the rendered frame's grain standard deviation at the default `grainSize` 2 and `grainHold` 2 with the default crunch 0.42 (EXPORT-1; the defaults are Q7).
  The threshold stands on measurements of that shape (`h264_grain4.txt`, Mac VideoToolbox): 46.9% at 28 Mbps and 42.8% at 25 Mbps without crunch, 46.5% at 28 Mbps through a JPEG q66 proxy for crunch 0.42; hold 3 measured 46.7% (2 px cells) and 55.0% (3 px), and 3 px cells with the crunch proxy 56.6 to 62.7%; per-pixel per-frame grain retains 15% (`h264_grain.txt`).
  The round-1 text cited "47 to 50% for that shape", which was hold 3 (`h264_grain3.txt`); the phone's own encoder is U12 (AUDIT-7, FRESH-4).
  Color is an acceptance row of its own (R5-FRESH-8): on both devices P8.2 decodes one exported frame and compares the mean color of three flat palette regions with the rendered frame, passing within an Oklab distance of 0.02 in each.
  The platform encoder converts the sRGB canvas to 4:2:0 YUV with a matrix and range this design does not set, and a player assumes its own; a mismatch would shift saturated hues or lift and crush tones, in a product whose point is saturated palette color, and no other row would see it (INFERRED that any mismatch exists; not measured).
  Each MP4 export logs `export.video` with the color space the first `VideoFrame` and the encoder's decoder configuration declared; on macOS WebKit a frame from a WebGL2 canvas declared null primaries, transfer, and matrix while the decoder configuration declared BT.709 primaries, the sRGB transfer, and full range (the interrogation's reviewer C, section 2), so the declarations can disagree on a correct export and the row compares decoded colors, reading both fields as inputs (R7-21); if the row fails, the fix is to state the color space where the platform allows it, in the muxer's color box or on a buffer-built `VideoFrame`, chosen from those recorded values (U13).
  4:2:0 chroma is inherent to this H.264 profile, so one-pixel glyph strokes and palette edges are softer in an MP4 than in the PNG; the owner judges that on the exported files.
- **GIF loop.** Render with `output: gif` and `frameCount(motion, 100 / delayCs)`: passes 1 to 5 at master size, box-downsample to the GIF size (480 long edge by default), then the camera stage on the GIF grid (grain in GIF pixels, clipping; no crunch for GIFs), sample every fifth frame to build one global 256-color table in Oklab seeded with the palette LUT's entries (JUDGE-A-8, JUDGE-B-13), map every pixel by exact nearest search in a worker, LZW in the same worker, frame delay `delayCs` (10 cs by default), NETSCAPE2.0 loop forever; the Export tab shows an estimated size before the owner commits, presents MP4 as the faithful format, and the defaults are the owner's (Q8, settled).
  **The estimate is a pause inside the GIF job** (R7-D3): once the palette is built from the sampled frames and frame 0 is mapped and LZW-encoded in the worker, the job reports progress `{ phase: 'estimate', bytes, frameCount }`, where `bytes` is the encoded frame 0 times the frame count plus the header, the global table, and the loop extension, logs `export.estimate`, and waits, still inside its export phase, for `confirm()` or `cancel()`; on `confirm()` it renders and encodes every frame with that same palette, so the palette is built once, and `cancel()` settles it `cancelled` with no file.
  The pause holds the export phase like any other moment of the job: input stays ignored, the driver stays held, hiding the tab settles it `hidden`, and `close()` settles it `cancelled`.
  The estimate needs frames rendered through pass 5 and the palette, which only the export phase may draw (rule 3 below), and its accuracy needs the palette the file will use, so a separate estimate call that rendered, sampled, and released would have drawn twice, and an estimate shown while the job ran on would have come after the owner committed, not before as Q8 says (section 21).
  A box-downsample of 2048 to 480 averages 2 px master grain cells to 0.47 px, so grain rendered before the downsample would vanish (FRESH-10); running the camera stage on the GIF grid is what keeps the signature grain in a GIF, at the cost that a GIF's grain cells are not the MP4's.
  The GIF's own frame count is what closes the loop: 8 cs (12.5 fps) over a 3 s loop is 37.5 frames of a 30 fps loop and cannot sample it seamlessly, whereas `round(loopSeconds * 100 / delayCs)` GIF frames with phase `frame / frameCount` close at any delay (AUDIT-12).
- With motion off, a loop is the still's scene with moving grain, which is still a seamless loop, and it keeps the still's framing: the camera is the identity whenever the effective orbit is 0, so such a loop's frame 0 is the still byte for byte, and a loop of moving objects with `orbit` 0 is the still outside the moving objects (Q19 a, settled; R4-FRESH-6).
- **Sharing.** Exports take seconds and the share sheet needs a fresh tap, so the export ends with a button: when `deviceClass` is `phone`, a Share button whose tap calls `navigator.share({ files })` if `canShare` accepts the file and downloads it otherwise; on the desktop, a Download button, always.
  Desktop Chrome and Safari expose `navigator.share` too, so `canShare` alone would open a share sheet on the Mac (R5-OBS-2).
- **An export is an exclusive phase of the session (AUDIT-6, R5-FRESH-3).** From the call to `exportStill` or `exportLoop` until the job settles, `activity` is `exporting`, and one set of rules covers every case:
  1. Input is ignored: `edit`, `undo`, `redo`, `play`, and `setIntent` do nothing, `exportScene` and a second export resolve `cancelled`, and `tap` resolves `failed` with the `cancelled` error; the Editor shows the job's progress and Cancel, and nothing else is enabled.
  2. The job waits, in its `waiting-analysis` phase, until analysis is **quiet**: `reconcile` lists no need, no worker is running a stage or a decode, and no tap is pending, so a tap made before the export, still waiting on the encoder, is applied first and resolves (R7-D4); a deferred plate refill (7.1 rule 4) runs at once.
     Failed and blocked needs are not listed (5.4), so a `failed` or `blocked-after-crashes` depth or plate does not hold the export: it proceeds without that product, as section 15 allows, and the Export tab says so ("Exporting without depth").
     The status chip shows the analysis state as usual, and cancel during the wait returns `cancelled`.
  3. From quiet until the job settles, the driver starts no worker and no model prefetch, no product is applied, and the scheduler draws only the job's frames; on leaving the wait the driver also releases a resident decoder if one is alive, so no analysis worker of any kind exists while frames are drawn, whatever tab the UI had active (R6-DEF-7), and the job logs `export.start` there and `export.end` when it settles (R6-OBS-1).
     The document changes only through edits and products, so nothing the renderer reads can change in that span and every frame of a loop is drawn from one scene: all of them include the automatic subject or none does.
     `encode` is not listed at quiet, so the phone's mode is full, and every export renders the full pass set at divisor 1; the dev-only `forceMode` lever of 5.10 does not reach an export's frames either (R7-9).
  4. Hiding the tab settles the job `hidden` in any phase, with no file; the Export tab says "Export stopped because noisy went to the background" and the owner exports again.
     Neither rendering nor the platform encoder is relied on while hidden (INFERRED: a VideoToolbox session may fail when Safari is backgrounded), and an interruption is never an encoder error.
  5. When the job settles, by success, error, `cancel()`, `hidden`, or `close()`, `activity` returns to `editing`, the canvas shows the still, and the driver reconciles again.

  Exports therefore never overlap an analysis worker, in either direction (rule 2 of 7.1): the quiet condition excludes a sequence worker and a decode in flight, rule 3 keeps them out until the job settles, and the decoder is either already gone, because the automatic subject's decoder is released after its one decode and the Objects tab's decoder when the tab is left (7.1, R6-DEF-7), or released by rule 3, so P8.1 asserts `alive.decoder` false at the job's first frame; exports never ship the doubled edge of a stale plate; the export chain is the preview chain, so no extra GPU targets exist beyond the encoder's own buffers, which P8 measures on the phone.

## 15. Failure and recovery

| Failure | Detection | Behavior | Recovery |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Model download fails (offline, HTTP error, stall) | `fetchModels` result | Stage `failed` with `download`; the look and every feature not needing that stage keep working; chip says "Couldn't download the depth model" with Retry | Retry button; `stalled` and `offline` retry once on `online`; Range resume from the temporary cache entry; nothing unverified is promoted |
| Downloaded model fails its SHA-256 | `integrity` | Bytes discarded; logged as `stage.failed` with `integrity` and the `ModelId`, which carries the expected hash (R5-OBS-3) | Retry |
| Worker throws inside a stage | `failed` reply or `error` event | That stage `failed` with `worker`; the sequence worker is terminated; later stages in the list are re-planned on the next reconcile | Retry; other stages continue |
| Stage exceeds its watchdog (visible time, session plus infer) | driver timer | `failed` with `timeout`; worker terminated | Retry; the fetch phase never counts |
| Tab hidden during analysis | `visibilitychange` | Save flushed at once; `hiddenAt` set in each occupied marker slot; watchdog paused; grace timer `hiddenGraceMs` (4 s); fired on time and still hidden: heavy job cancelled and its worker terminated, decoder released, both marker slots cleared; fired late (the process was suspended): re-armed, nothing cancelled; chip `paused-hidden` | Visible inside the grace, or after a suspension the job survived: nothing lost, the watchdog resumes and the job finishes; visible after a cancel: reconcile reruns the missing stage silently after that stage's cooldown; no failure or crash recorded (R3-FRESH-2) |
| Tab killed in the foreground mid-stage (jetsam) | a liveness marker, in the heavy or the decode slot, with no live tab lock and no `hiddenAt` | Count for (scene, op, build), each slot scored by itself, so a kill during a decode beside a refill counts against both ops (R4-FRESH-5); completed products were saved | Reconcile reruns; after two counts the op is `blocked-after-crashes` for that scene with "Analysis crashed this tab twice. Try again?" and a laptop suggestion |
| Tab reclaimed in the background (killed inside the hidden grace) | marker with `hiddenAt` set | Marker deleted, no count | Reconcile reruns silently |
| Second tab while a job runs | marker whose tab lock is still held | Marker left alone | None |
| Reload or navigation while a job runs | `pagehide` | Save flushed; both marker slots cleared, so the released tab lock is never read as a crash | Reconcile reruns on the next open |
| Export requested while analysis is not quiet | the export phase's wait (section 14) | Job phase `waiting-analysis`; a deferred plate refill runs now; chip shows the stage; input is ignored | Export proceeds when `reconcile` lists nothing and nothing is in flight, and from then on the driver starts nothing until the job settles; cancel during the wait returns `cancelled`; a failed or blocked depth exports without depth and the Export tab says so |
| Tab hidden during an export | `visibilitychange` | The job settles `hidden` in any phase, with no file and no retry; the Export tab says why (R5-FRESH-3) | Export again |
| Edit, tap, play, or a second export during an export | `activity` is `exporting` | Ignored; `exportScene` and the second export resolve `cancelled`, and `tap` resolves `failed` with the `cancelled` error; the Editor shows only progress and Cancel | Cancel the job, or wait for it |
| Storage quota on save | `QuotaExceededError` | Document stays in memory; `evictCaches`; retry once; banner; saves retry on each edit | Export this scene (from memory, complete) or delete scenes |
| Import stops part-way (quota, a crash, a killed tab) | `createScene`'s transaction aborts | Nothing is written: no record and no asset; the import error names quota when that is the cause (R4-FRESH-8) | Import again, after freeing space if quota was the cause |
| Storage quota on an asset write | `putAsset` result | The produce or tap fails typed with `quota`; the document does not reference the asset; chip or `TapResult` says so | Same as above |
| IndexedDB connection lost | `UnknownError` or `InvalidStateError` | Reopen and retry once; then `unavailable` with "Storage stopped responding; export this scene or reload" | Each edit retries; `exportScene` assembles from the in-memory document and the asset cache and needs no store read, so the banner's advice is always actionable (FRESH-5) |
| Local record newer than this build | `peekVersion` | Listed as `newer` with the writer build; only Export scene file offered, through the verbatim path (stored JSON untouched, every asset in the key range) | The newer build reopens the exported file; or reload noisy at the newer build |
| Local record damaged | strict parse after migration | Listed as `damaged`; "Export raw data" (the verbatim path: a zip any unzip tool opens, with the stored PNG and JPEG assets readable) first, "Delete" second | Manual |
| Unknown keys at a known version | strict parse after `peekVersion` | Listed as `damaged` with the key paths in the detail; never opened, rewritten, or swept (13.4 rule 5, R5-FRESH-9) | "Export raw data" keeps every field and every asset in the key range, and the build that wrote the record opens that file |
| WebGL2, both float color-buffer extensions, a limit, or a complete render target missing | `createRenderer` at boot: `no-webgl2`, `missing-extension`, `limit`, or `incomplete` with the name (9.1, R7-F1) | Library works and exports scene files; editor shows the missing capability; the environment snapshot carries the report | None needed on the target devices |
| GL context lost | `webglcontextlost` | `recovering`; rebuild from decoded assets on restore; exports in flight fail with `renderer` | After three losses in 60 s, "The GPU stopped responding. Reload noisy." while saving continues |
| Corrupt, foreign, or repacked scene file | `readSceneFile` | `not-a-scene`, `too-large`, `newer-version`, `corrupt`, or `repacked-unsupported` with a plain sentence; nothing written | Re-export from the source device, or update noisy |
| Scene open in another tab | Web Lock unavailable | "This scene is open in another tab. Close it there first." | Close the other tab, Open again |
| H.264 refused at the master size | `isConfigSupported` | MP4 hidden with the reason; GIF and PNG remain; there is no smaller size to fall back to (section 14, R7-C) | None; a refusal on the phone (U13) reopens a fallback only with a video-grid render output |
| Encoder error mid-export | `VideoEncoder.error` | The job fails with `encoder-unsupported` and the encoder's message; nothing is retried at another bitrate (R5-FRESH-3) | Export again, at a lower bitrate if the message points that way |
| Auto subject finds nothing | the coverage and edge gate of 5.5 (R7-D2) | No object; `autoSubject` done; note in the Objects tab | Tap to create the subject by hand |
| Every candidate of a tap's decode is empty (no texel at or above 128) | the driver's emptiness rule (7.1 item 2, R6-3) | `pick`, `new`, and `refine` resolve `empty` and write nothing; a `cycle` whose other two candidates are empty resolves `selected` and applies nothing; the Objects tab says nothing was found there | Tap elsewhere, or add an include point |
| A tap waits on the encoder and cannot be applied: the `encode` stage fails or is blocked, or the session closes | the driver's tap queue (5.9, R7-D4) | The tap's pending promise settles `failed` with the encoder's error, `blocked-after-crashes`, or `failed` with `cancelled` on close; no object is added | Retry the encoder, then tap again |
| Depth failed or blocked | `analysis.depth === null` after a failed or blocked op | Look renders without depth edges; Motion and Lights show why they are disabled; no automatic subject is guessed without depth, the Objects tab says so, and the owner's first tap defines the subject (R3-FRESH-1) | Retry, or open the scene on the laptop; its products then travel in the file |
| Not cross-origin isolated (misconfigured host) | `crossOriginIsolated === false` | ORT runs single-threaded (slower, correct); diagnostics record it | Fix the `headers.json` deployment |
| Stale plate (objects changed while inpainting) | `forMasks` mismatch on arrival | Dropped | `plateNeeded` schedules the current set |

Analysis failures never block the look, and a scene file always carries whatever products exist, so the laptop can finish what the phone could not.

## 16. Edge cases that shaped the design

- No subject found (landscapes, flat scenes): nothing is protected until the owner taps; the gate of 5.5 rejects masks under 2% or over 60% of the frame or touching three or four frame edges, a mask touching an edge when a texel at or above 128 lies in that edge's outermost row or column, so a near ground plane, which touches the bottom and both sides, is rejected rather than outlined as the subject (R7-D2); `autoSubject` becomes `done` either way so it never resurrects (JUDGE-B-11), and the owner's first object sets it `done` too, so deleting or undoing that object later never brings a guess (R4-FRESH-11).
- The owner taps the background (sky, wall): it becomes an object and a layer; the plate behind it is poor but shows only in thin bands; the palette swatch (Q4 a) is the path for "make the blue bright blue".
- Nested objects (a shirt inside a person): the contained object draws after its container and wins the pixels in `labelAt`, so the part moves and recolors on its own.
- A tap before the encoder has finished: it is queued and its promise stays pending until the decoder applies it, in tap order, so the UI learns the created object, an `empty` outcome, or a failure from the same call; the chip reads "Getting objects ready" from `state.analysis` while the tap waits (R7-D4).
  An export started meanwhile waits for the tap to be applied, so the export holds everything the owner did before it; closing the scene settles the tap `failed` with `cancelled`.
- Taps during playback: there are none.
  The Objects tab stops playback and shows the still for as long as it is active, and anywhere else a touch on a playing canvas stops the loop onto the still and does nothing more, so every tap is resolved on a frame where view space is frame space and `labelAt` over the stored masks is exact for every layer.
  A loop frame displaces layers away from the anchor by up to `PARALLAX_MARGIN` plus their own motion, about 41 px at 2048, and no single inverse mapping undoes that for every layer (R3-FRESH-3).
- Very small cells: `cellSize` is even and at least 6 master px, so glyphs stay legible and cells are exact at master size; partial edge cells are symmetric because the grid is centered.
- Grayscale photos: every cluster is neutral, so the palette is `colors` gray entries spread over the photo's tone range, each pixel goes to the nearest in lightness, and the chroma weight leaves saturation and harmony nothing to move; recolor still works because it is a target mapping (R5-FRESH-1).
- Portrait orientation, panoramas (long edge 2048, short edge possibly about 500 px), screenshots with alpha (flattened on black): all representable down to a master short side of 64 px, below which a strip is refused as `too-small` before any decode (section 12, R7-19).
  Whatever the photo's shape, both master sides are even (section 12), so a 3:2 crop or an odd panorama reaches the MP4 encoder at its own size and is never scaled (R7-C).
  A panorama costs depth resolution, not memory: the token bound of 5.2 gives a 4:1 master a 1190 x 294 depth input, so its depth stage stays at the 4:3 figure where the unbounded input measured 1103 MB of wasm, and its depth map is coarser across the short side (R5-FRESH-4).
  The SAM encoder takes every master stretched to its fixed 1024 square, as the bench did, so a 4:1 panorama reaches it squeezed four to one; what that does to mask quality is not measured, and the panorama in the owner's test set is judged with the others at P4 (U16).
- An object touching the frame edge with motion: `objectMotionBound` scales its amplitude toward zero along that axis so no cut edge slides into the crop.
- A light behind an object (light depth greater than the object's): the march compares interpolated depth along the 2D ray, so shadows fall toward the camera side; lights may sit off-frame; a light behind the subject gives silhouette rays.
- Changing loop duration: cycles are integers per loop, so the loop stays seamless; camera and object phases rescale.
- Deleting an object: the reducer drops it and nothing else, so a stored `motion.focus` stays the disparity it was (R7-D6), and `plateNeeded` schedules a refill when the loop needs a plate; the refill is deferred while the Objects tab is active, so a run of edits there costs one refill when the tab is left; the refill runs in full mode, and if the owner returns to the Objects tab while it runs, the decoder respawns and answers taps without waiting for it (AUDIT-2, R3-AUDIT-4).
- A loop at `orbit` 0 with no object motion: the camera is the identity and no layer moves, so no hole is ever uncovered and `plateNeeded` lists no plate, where round 6 ran an inpaint sequence for it (R6-8, 5.4).
- Motion turned on before the plate exists: the back layer is the master for the second or so the inpaint takes (plus the encoder cooldown, the first time after import), so moving objects briefly show a doubled edge in the revealed band; the chip shows the wait, and an export started in that window waits for the plate rather than shipping the edge (AUDIT-6).
- An export started while analysis is still running, or inside an encoder cooldown: it waits until analysis is quiet, which right after an import can be the whole sequence and the automatic subject; the Export tab shows the wait with Cancel, and no worker starts once the frames are being drawn (section 14, R5-FRESH-3).
- A short interruption mid-encoder (a notification pull-down, a call banner): inside the 4 s hidden grace nothing is lost; once the grace has cancelled the job, the stage's elapsed work and its cooldown are the cost, and the chip says so (AUDIT-5); an app switch that suspends the process before the grace fires cancels nothing, and the job finishes on return (R3-FRESH-2).
- Scenes moving between Mac Safari and Mac Chrome: separate storage per browser, so the scene file is the path even on one machine.
- A second photo imported while analysis runs: the first scene's session closes and its analysis stops; it resumes when that scene reopens.
  The new scene's first heavy worker waits out the cooldown of the worker just terminated, because the termination record belongs to the page's analysis service and not to the closed session (7.1 rule 3, R4-FRESH-4).
- Undo after analysis: undo restores the editable projection only; depth, palettes, the plate, and every surviving object's current mask stay; a restored object brings back its mask (JUDGE-A-3).
- Two sliders within 600 ms: coalesce keys are per field, so each slider keeps its own undo step.
- A palette target on a merged entry: the target follows the entry's heaviest cluster, so raising `colors` until that cluster's entry splits leaves the target on the split that holds the cluster and returns the other pixels to their own colors; lowering `colors` until two targeted clusters share an entry renders the lower-numbered target, which is then the only one of the two that anchors the harmony, and the other returns, as a color and as an anchor, when they split again (section 10 Palette targets, R6-2).
- A flat photo or a screenshot with fewer than 16 distinct colors: `analysis.colors` has fewer than 16 clusters (section 12), the strip shows that many unpinned swatches, the reducer refuses a target on a cluster the photo does not have, and `parseDocument` lists a record or file whose target names one as `invalid`, like any other broken invariant of 5.2 (R6-2).
- A tap decoded during a depth retry (depth failed, the owner tapped, then Retry): its object lands with `produced.depth` null, and the driver's state-based rule fills its statistics after the next apply once depth exists, so it sorts by its real depth and, as the subject, anchors the loop (R7-D8).
- An `object.depth` product that reorders two overlapping objects changes no asset, and the label texture, the normals, and the simplify cache are keyed by the ordered list of (object id, mask asset id), so the next render rebuilds them over those objects' bounds and equals a fresh open's (9.2, R7-D7).
- A repeated tap on the selected object with no candidates in memory (the scene was reopened, or another object was decoded since): the cycle decodes the stored selection first, about 0.1 s, then applies the next candidate; the stored `candidate` index is what makes the same tap sequence give the same mask on both devices (Q5 a).
- Video later: a clip becomes `frames: Frame[]` with per-frame analysis and the same clip-wide style, symbols, motion, and lights; `ObjectProduced.mask` becomes a per-frame track; the renderer already takes one frame's assets and every clip-wide quantity comes from the document, so this is a version bump with a migration that wraps the single frame, not a rewrite.

## 17. Compatibility classification

| Surface | Class | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Scene file format | COMPATIBILITY REQUIRED (backward) from the freeze, the first keeper scene at version 1 (Q18, settled); before it, a version-0 scene that stops parsing is made again from its photo | user-owned durable contract that moves across devices and time; newer files are refused with a message (forward compatibility not required); a newer local record is still exportable verbatim so no build ever strands data it cannot read (AUDIT-4) |
| IndexedDB records | MIGRATION REQUIRED | same `parseDocument` chain as files; records rewritten on the next save; newer records listed, never destroyed |
| IndexedDB schema (stores and indexes) | MIGRATION REQUIRED | `onupgradeneeded` with a data-preserving migration; version integer independent of `SCENE_VERSION` |
| Committed look semantics (section 10) | COMPATIBILITY REQUIRED | a stored number's meaning is part of the format; a semantic change bumps the version with a migration mapping old values where a mapping exists; where none exists the change is documented in an ADR and exports remain the record (Q13, settled) |
| Analysis product shapes (depth, mask resolution) | MIGRATION REQUIRED if changed | in the document; a change nulls the product for recompute |
| Model and embeddings caches | NOT APPLICABLE | disposable, keyed by lock hash and `ModelId`, recomputable |
| Serving origin (tailnet now, AWS later) | MIGRATION REQUIRED at release | storage is per origin; the migration is scene-file export and import |
| Hosting | COMPATIBILITY REQUIRED | the bundle stays static with the `headers.json` contract, which S3 plus CloudFront provides through a response-headers policy |
| Browser APIs | NOT APPLICABLE (no existing users) | minimums: WebGL2 with `EXT_color_buffer_float` or `EXT_color_buffer_half_float` and complete 16-bit float render targets (9.1, R7-F1), OffscreenCanvas 2D, WebCodecs, `CompressionStream` deflate-raw, Web Locks, Web Share files, SharedArrayBuffer under COOP and COEP, `createImageBitmap` resize options |
| Existing code, APIs, configs | NOT APPLICABLE | greenfield repository with no commits |
| Bench and spike harnesses | NOT APPLICABLE | throwaway experiments; `experiments/iphone-bench/scene.js` is promoted into `test/fixtures/synth/` as a fixture generator (TEST-4) by P1.2, the first part whose rows read it (section 22, R7-B4); the two archives of `experiments/zip-rezip/` stay zip-level fixtures for P2c.1 only (13.4) |
## 18. Dependencies with ownership-cost evaluations

ONNX Runtime Web is approved (`decisions.tsv`, gate3 row 2026-09-29T02:43:27Z), and the rest of the table as listed (Q1, gate3 row 2026-09-30T17:51:40Z): `preact`, `zod`, and `mediabunny` at runtime, `typescript` 7.0.2 and `esbuild` in dev, and the three model weight sets; `@types/node` joined the dev list on 2026-10-01, pinned exactly (Q1 as amended, gate3 row 2026-10-01T03:29:44Z, R7-A1); the rejected list stands.
Each was weighed on maturity, maintenance activity, security posture, dependency surface, replaceability, and the size of the in-repo code that would replace it, with neither building nor buying as the default.
Versions were VERIFIED on the npm registry on 2026-09-28 and 2026-09-29, and the dev tools again on 2026-10-01; P0.1 pins exactly the versions Q1 approved, not whatever is newest when it runs (R7-E): `preact` 10.29.8, `zod` 4.6.5, `mediabunny` 1.60.0, and `onnxruntime-web` 1.30.0 at runtime; `typescript` 7.0.2, `esbuild` 0.28.2 (the newest patch of the approved 0.28 line), and `@types/node` 25.9.8 (the newest 25.x, matching Node 25) in dev.
Newer releases exist and are not taken: Preact 11.0.0 shipped on 2026-09-30, a major version with breaking changes (by its upgrade guide as the interrogation read it: numeric style values lose their automatic `px` with no type error, `defaultProps` moved to `preact/compat`, builds are ESM-only), and Mediabunny 1.61.0 on 2026-09-29; moving to either is a separate, later decision.

| Need | Recommendation | Evaluation | Runner-up |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Model runtime | `onnxruntime-web` 1.30.0, exact, plain wasm build only (approved 2026-09-29) | MIT, Microsoft; only `ort.wasm.min.mjs` and the threaded wasm ship, served same-origin; its npm dependencies stay in the build tree | none |
